{\LARGE\bfseries QAOA: methods and congestion atlas\par}
\subsection*{Step 1 | Audited QAOA study of 26 October 2026}
Purpose. Understand how the mapped QAOA circuit becomes routed communication, where those routes concentrate demand, and how the structural congestion changes as capacity increases. This edition covers methods and congestion only. Physical simulation comparisons and dynamic-policy results are reserved for the next step.\par
\section*{1. Circuit to placement}
The input is a 48-qubit QAOA circuit in OpenQASM. The architecture has 16 cores arranged in a 4 x 4 mesh or torus. A mesh connects neighbouring grid positions; a torus also connects opposite boundaries. The current experiment selects T-HQA, with mapping seeds 42, 43 and 44.\par
T-HQA is the topology-aware HQA implementation. It constructs assignment costs from shortest-hop distances and uses lookahead attraction from future interactions. A Hungarian assignment solver selects assignments from the feasible cost matrix. This encourages interacting qubits to meet while accounting for transfer distance; it does not directly minimise the congestion percentages defined later.\par
The recorded mapper configuration uses OEE initial placement, four data-qubit slots per core, and link-reliability values in the range 0.1 to 1.0. Initial-placement implementation: \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/mapper/qmap/placement/initial.py}}{\nolinkurl{mapper/qmap/placement/initial.py}}. Data-qubit placement capacity is distinct from the communication-memory threshold C swept in the congestion study.\par
Implementation: \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/mapper/qmap/placement/T_HQA.py}}{\nolinkurl{mapper/qmap/placement/T_HQA.py}}; \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/mapper/qmap/placement/cost.py}}{\nolinkurl{mapper/qmap/placement/cost.py}}; \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/mapper/qmap/placement/assignment.py}}{\nolinkurl{mapper/qmap/placement/assignment.py}}.\par
The QAOA run imports previously generated placements and routes from the audited golden-study corpus. It does not rerun placement during each policy simulation. Placement contracts record assignments[t][q], the core occupied by qubit q at slice t. Reusing a fixed trace lets scheduler comparisons share the same communication input.\par
Mapping orchestration: \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/tools/golden_study/map_inputs.py}}{\nolinkurl{tools/golden_study/map_inputs.py}}. Placement contract: \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/tools/mapper_ace/placement_contract.py}}{\nolinkurl{tools/mapper_ace/placement_contract.py}}. Audited QAOA import: \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/tools/golden_study/prepare_qaoa.py}}{\nolinkurl{tools/golden_study/prepare_qaoa.py}}.\par
Input and provenance: \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/results/qaoa_2seed_2026-10-26/inputs/qaoa/circuit.qasm}}{\nolinkurl{results/qaoa_2seed_2026-10-26/inputs/qaoa/circuit.qasm}}; \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/results/qaoa_2seed_2026-10-26/mapping_contract.json}}{\nolinkurl{results/qaoa_2seed_2026-10-26/mapping_contract.json}}; \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/results/qaoa_2seed_2026-10-26/validation/mapping_audit.csv}}{\nolinkurl{results/qaoa_2seed_2026-10-26/validation/mapping_audit.csv}}.\par

\section*{2. Placement to physical routes}
A placement change gives a logical migration: a qubit moves from one core to another at a compiler layer. The adapter assigns a path to that migration, checks its endpoints and physical adjacency, and exports adjacent-hop requests. Routing changes the path while preserving the required placement transitions.\par
\begingroup\small\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}\begin{center}\begin{tabular}{lrr}\toprule
Topology & Route & Decision rule \\
\midrule
Mesh & XY & Coordinate dimension 0, then dimension 1 \\
Mesh & YX & Coordinate dimension 1, then dimension 0 \\
Mesh & shortest\_reliable & Shortest hops; reliability breaks ties \\
Mesh & max\_reliability & Maximum product reliability; hops break ties \\
Torus & dijkstra & Shortest hops with reliability tie-breaks \\
\bottomrule\end{tabular}\end{center}\endgroup
XY and YX test how equal-length paths redistribute load. Reliability-aware paths can choose different relays; maximum reliability may accept extra hops. The torus tests a different connectivity graph as well as its approved routing policy, so mesh-versus-torus differences cannot be attributed to routing alone.\par
Implementation: \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/tools/mapper_ace/routing.py}}{\nolinkurl{tools/mapper_ace/routing.py}}; \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/mapper/qmap/metrics/distance.py}}{\nolinkurl{mapper/qmap/metrics/distance.py}}; migration construction and validation in \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/tools/mapper_ace/adapter.py}}{\nolinkurl{tools/mapper_ace/adapter.py}}. The oracle also uses EPR latency as a later tie-breaker when hop and reliability costs tie.\par
There are 15 conceptual route aliases: (4 mesh routes + 1 torus route) x 3 seeds. Only 11 request files have distinct SHA-256 hashes. Identical files remain visible as separate route labels in this atlas, but they are not independent observations. Simulation planning deduplicates exact traces.\par
\section*{3. Routed traces to ACE experiments}
The broader study compares on-demand generation (ODG), continuous generation (CGP), adaptive continuous generation (ACGP), and Dynamic full-chain/first-hop preparation. It sweeps total memories 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 16 and 32, legal compiler allocations, and evaluation physics seeds 0 and 1. These simulation dimensions do not multiply the trace-only congestion atlas.\par

\clearpage
\section*{4. What congestion means here}
For each communication-active compiler layer l and core c, let D(c,l) be total route demand and E(c,l) the demand from route endpoints. Capacity C is a structural threshold. Overload requires D > C; equality is not overload. Empty communication layers are excluded from the denominator A.\par
\textbf{Hop counting:} a source or destination contributes one demand unit; each internal relay contributes two (arrival and departure). \textbf{Route counting:} each distinct core visited by a qubit route contributes one unit. Both views are included because relay accounting changes the structural pressure estimate.\par
Example: path C0 - C1 - C2 contributes [1,2,1] in hop counting and [1,1,1] in route counting. At C=1, C1 is overloaded only under hop counting. This demonstrates why the two definitions cannot be pooled as if they measured the same resource model.\par
\textbf{Total overload:} 100 x count(D > C) / A. \textbf{Endpoint overload:} 100 x count(E > C) / A. \textbf{Relay-only overload:} 100 x count(E <= C < D) / A. Endpoint overload and relay-only overload partition the total-overload layers for each core; their percentages add to total.\par
Relay-only does not mean the core has no endpoint traffic. It means endpoint demand alone fits, but additional relay demand pushes the total above capacity. Endpoint overload also does not exclude simultaneous relay traffic.\par
The trace-level statistic is different: percentage of active layers with \emph{any} overloaded core. It is not the mean of the 16 per-core percentages. The overview graphs use this any-core measure; the spatial heatmaps use per-core percentages.\par
Why sweep capacity? Increasing C while holding the trace fixed isolates the threshold sensitivity of the same demand pattern. Overload must be non-increasing with C. Zero overload means demand stays within this structural threshold, not that the physical simulator has no waiting, retries, or fidelity loss.\par
These figures do not simulate occupancy, link timing, memory expiry, or dynamic cache state. They cannot establish a winning scheduler or a latency speedup. Mapping seeds vary compiler inputs; physics seeds do not enter this calculation. Three seed values are shown individually rather than treated as strong statistical evidence.\par
Analysis implementation: \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/tools/golden_study/analyze_qft96_congestion.py}}{\nolinkurl{tools/golden_study/analyze_qft96_congestion.py}}. Its historical filename refers to QFT, but this PDF verifies QAOA CSV rows and hashes and regenerates QAOA-labelled figures. Sources: \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/results/qaoa_2seed_2026-10-26/congestion/per_core_congestion.csv}}{\nolinkurl{results/qaoa_2seed_2026-10-26/congestion/per_core_congestion.csv}} and \href{\detokenize{file:///home/dhruv-pansuriya/Documents/BTP/Compiler-Driven-ACE/results/qaoa_2seed_2026-10-26/congestion/trace_congestion_summary.csv}}{\nolinkurl{results/qaoa_2seed_2026-10-26/congestion/trace_congestion_summary.csv}}.\par

% BEGIN QAOA METRIC RESULTS
\clearpage
\part*{QAOA physical-simulation results}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{QAOA physical-simulation results}
\section*{How to read the metric results}
The validated base study is complete: 1,639 of 1,639 jobs and 6,402 of 6,402 policy-seed evaluations. The sections first report mesh results and then torus results. Every graph uses total memory banks per core on the horizontal axis and contains the applicable methodology lines: ODG, CGP, ACGP, Dynamic full-chain, and Dynamic first-hop.
Each source simulation cell already averages evaluation physics seeds 0 and 1. A plotted point then averages all validated mapping-trace cells and all legal compiler-memory allocations available for its topology, route, total-memory value, and policy. This allocation-averaged view answers the requested total-memory question without selecting a favourable compiler split. The table reports the number of cells and the included compiler allocations so unequal cell counts remain visible. The shaded-error convention is omitted because two physics seeds do not support a strong uncertainty claim; the companion CSV nevertheless records the standard deviation across plotted cells.
ODG uses compiler allocation zero. The other policies are evaluated over legal compiler allocations. Exact byte-identical route traces were executed once, so XY/YX cells can represent aliases from several mapping seeds while reliability-aware routes may contain three distinct trace groups. Mapping seeds and compiler allocations are controlled inputs, not independent experimental replications.
All blank or inapplicable values are shown as ``--. Compiler-planner, compiler-cache, hit, fallback, and storage counters are defined only for Dynamic full-chain and Dynamic first-hop; drawing zero lines for ODG, CGP, or ACGP would falsely imply those methods ran the Dynamic compiler planner.
Every table follows its graph and links the exact compact CSV used for both. Mean is the plotted value; SD is descriptive variation across mapping-trace/allocation cells, not a confidence interval. The master validated source is \href{run:../../../results/qaoa_2seed_2026-10-26/summaries/base-evaluation-progress.csv}{base-evaluation-progress.csv}.
\clearpage
\part*{Mesh results}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Mesh results}
The mesh section contains four routing panels per figure: XY, YX, shortest reliable, and maximum reliability. Route choice changes relay paths while the topology remains the 4 by 4 mesh.
\clearpage
\section*{Latency and waiting}
\clearpage
\subsection*{Mean request latency}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{\detokenize{figures/results/mesh__request_latency_mean_ms.png}}\end{center}
This plot asks how mean request latency changes when total communication memory per core increases. Each point is the arithmetic mean of the validated cells listed in the table; the graph does not select a best compiler allocation. Read comparisons vertically at a fixed memory value and route, then inspect the full capacity trend. Lower values indicate faster service; mean, median, p95, and maximum are kept separate because they describe different parts of the latency distribution.

\subsection*{Median request latency}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{\detokenize{figures/results/mesh__request_latency_median_ms.png}}\end{center}
This plot asks how median request latency changes when total communication memory per core increases. Each point is the arithmetic mean of the validated cells listed in the table; the graph does not select a best compiler allocation. Read comparisons vertically at a fixed memory value and route, then inspect the full capacity trend. Lower values indicate faster service; mean, median, p95, and maximum are kept separate because they describe different parts of the latency distribution.

\subsection*{Maximum request latency}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{\detokenize{figures/results/mesh__request_latency_max_ms.png}}\end{center}
This plot asks how maximum request latency changes when total communication memory per core increases. Each point is the arithmetic mean of the validated cells listed in the table; the graph does not select a best compiler allocation. Read comparisons vertically at a fixed memory value and route, then inspect the full capacity trend. Lower values indicate faster service; mean, median, p95, and maximum are kept separate because they describe different parts of the latency distribution.
\par
\href{run:tables/results/mesh__request_latency_max_ms.csv}{Exact graph/table CSV: \nolinkurl{mesh__request_latency_max_ms.csv}}.\par

\subsection*{Circuit makespan}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{\detokenize{figures/results/mesh__end_to_end_latency_ms.png}}\end{center}
This plot asks how circuit makespan changes when total communication memory per core increases. Each point is the arithmetic mean of the validated cells listed in the table; the graph does not select a best compiler allocation. Read comparisons vertically at a fixed memory value and route, then inspect the full capacity trend. Makespan covers the whole circuit execution and is reported in seconds; it is different from per-request latency.
\par
\href{run:tables/results/mesh__end_to_end_latency_ms.csv}{Exact graph/table CSV: \nolinkurl{mesh__end_to_end_latency_ms.csv}}.\par

\subsection*{Mean admission wait}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{\detokenize{figures/results/mesh__average_admission_wait_ms.png}}\end{center}
This plot asks how mean admission wait changes when total communication memory per core increases. Each point is the arithmetic mean of the validated cells listed in the table; the graph does not select a best compiler allocation. Read comparisons vertically at a fixed memory value and route, then inspect the full capacity trend. Lower values indicate faster service; mean, median, p95, and maximum are kept separate because they describe different parts of the latency distribution.

\subsection*{Requests that waited}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{\detokenize{figures/results/mesh__requests_that_waited_fraction.png}}\end{center}
This plot asks how requests that waited changes when total communication memory per core increases. Each point is the arithmetic mean of the validated cells listed in the table; the graph does not select a best compiler allocation. Read comparisons vertically at a fixed memory value and route, then inspect the full capacity trend. The fraction ranges from zero to one and counts requests whose admission wait crossed the study threshold.
\par
\href{run:tables/results/mesh__requests_that_waited_fraction.csv}{Exact graph/table CSV: \nolinkurl{mesh__requests_that_waited_fraction.csv}}.\par

\subsection*{Mean reservation setup time}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{\detokenize{figures/results/mesh__avg_reservation_setup_time_ms.png}}\end{center}
This plot asks how mean reservation setup time changes when total communication memory per core increases. Each point is the arithmetic mean of the validated cells listed in the table; the graph does not select a best compiler allocation. Read comparisons vertically at a fixed memory value and route, then inspect the full capacity trend.
\par

\section*{Delivered quality}
\clearpage
\subsection*{Mean delivered EPR fidelity}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{\detokenize{figures/results/mesh__average_delivered_epr_fidelity.png}}\end{center}
This plot asks how mean delivered epr fidelity changes when total communication memory per core increases. Each point is the arithmetic mean of the validated cells listed in the table; the graph does not select a best compiler allocation. Read comparisons vertically at a fixed memory value and route, then inspect the full capacity trend. Higher fidelity is desirable, but its tradeoff with latency, retries, and storage time must be read from the neighbouring figures.
\par
\href{run:tables/results/mesh__average_delivered_epr_fidelity.csv}{Exact graph/table CSV: \nolinkurl{mesh__average_delivered_epr_fidelity.csv}}.\par

\section*{Retries and robustness}
\clearpage
\subsection*{Total retries}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{\detokenize{figures/results/mesh__total_retries.png}}\end{center}
This plot asks how total retries changes when total communication memory per core increases. Each point is the arithmetic mean of the validated cells listed in the table; the graph does not select a best compiler allocation. Read comparisons vertically at a fixed memory value and route, then inspect the full capacity trend.
\par

\subsection*{Protocol-used pairs}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{\detokenize{figures/results/mesh__protocol_used_pairs.png}}\end{center}
This plot asks how protocol-used pairs changes when total communication memory per core increases. Each point is the arithmetic mean of the validated cells listed in the table; the graph does not select a best compiler allocation. Read comparisons vertically at a fixed memory value and route, then inspect the full capacity trend.
\par

\subsection*{Protocol-unused pairs}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{\detokenize{figures/results/mesh__protocol_unused_pairs.png}}\end{center}
This plot asks how protocol-unused pairs changes when total communication memory per core increases. Each point is the arithmetic mean of the validated cells listed in the table; the graph does not select a best compiler allocation. Read comparisons vertically at a fixed memory value and route, then inspect the full capacity trend. Lower values indicate less unused or expired work, subject to the paired latency and hit-rate results.

\section*{Dynamic compiler-planner and cache accounting}

\subsection*{Scheduled compiler preparations}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{\detokenize{figures/results/mesh__scheduled_preparations.png}}\end{center}
This plot asks how scheduled compiler preparations changes when total communication memory per core increases. Each point is the arithmetic mean of the validated cells listed in the table; the graph does not select a best compiler allocation. Read comparisons vertically at a fixed memory value and route, then inspect the full capacity trend. This counter exists only for Dynamic full-chain and Dynamic first-hop; the other methodologies are N/A by design.

\subsection*{Pregeneration success rate}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{\detokenize{figures/results/mesh__pregenerated_success_rate.png}}\end{center}
This plot asks how pregeneration success rate changes when total communication memory per core increases. Each point is the arithmetic mean of the validated cells listed in the table; the graph does not select a best compiler allocation. Read comparisons vertically at a fixed memory value and route, then inspect the full capacity trend. This counter exists only for Dynamic full-chain and Dynamic first-hop; the other methodologies are N/A by design.

\part*{Torus results}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Torus results}
The torus section uses the approved Dijkstra route on the 4 by 4 wraparound topology. Compare torus with mesh cautiously because both connectivity and mapped placements can differ.

\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{\detokenize{figures/results/torus__request_latency_mean_ms.png}}\end{center}

Lower values indicate faster service; mean, median, p95, and maximum are kept separate because they describe different parts of the latency distribution.

\subsection*{Maximum admission wait}

Each point is the arithmetic mean of the validated cells listed in the table; the graph does not select a best compiler allocation. Lower values indicate faster service; mean, median, p95, and maximum are kept separate because they describe different parts of the latency distribution.
